A page Safari suspended gets the events it missed when it resumes, which is on the way of a switch to it. Each tab event that changed the state was written on its own. A hidden tab loading a page sends its address, title, icon and load one after another, so 3–5 writes. A chat or timer whose title ticks every second wrote once a second for as long as it ran: about 40 ms/s across 30 tabs (estimated).
- **Changed:**
  - `store.js mutate` takes `within(state)`: how long the write may wait. The change goes into the state at once, in order with every other change. Only its write waits. It goes out with the first write after it of any kind, or when its time is up. So a write made later (a switch, a tab opened or closed, a pin) always carries every change made before it. Nothing is reordered, and a closed tab can't come back from an older update.
  - New `background/tab-updates.js` decides the wait for `tabs.onUpdated` (title, address, icon, a page loaded). A tab Margin has behind waits 300 ms (`GATHER_MS`), so a page loading behind is two writes: its new address at once (below), then its title, icon and load together. Its title alone is written at most once every 2 s (`TITLE_EVERY_MS`), per tab. The tab in front, and a tab Margin doesn't know yet, are written at once. So are a tab Safari says is active while Margin has it behind, or the other way around: either one saying it's in front is enough.
  - A hidden tab's new address is written at once too, whether it comes in the event or only in the tab with its load (a redirect). After a Safari restart or a Margin update every tab has a new id, and Margin matches tabs to their records by address (`model.js matchTabs`). A new address lost with the background stopping would leave the tab unmatched, in its window's active space instead of its own. A new fragment alone still waits: matching ignores it, and some sites change it often.
  - The waits are counted with `performance.now()`, a clock that only runs forward. With `Date.now()`, the Mac's clock set back (by hand, or synced after a sleep) held a title back by as much, and one set forward kept a later change waiting for an earlier, longer wait.
  - Activation, new tabs, closed tabs and every other change write at once, as before. A change with nothing new no longer writes the waiting changes early.
  - `stateRev` (what the background passes when it prepares a page) still names the last write while only changes that can wait are waiting. Storage has the write before them, the same as the page, so a read would get the same thing. Once their write is on its way (its time is up, or another write takes them), no write is known until it's through, as before.
  - Chrome: the same. It has the same storage events and hidden tabs that load.
- **Expected:** a page loading behind costs two writes, not 3–5. A ticking title costs one write per 2 s, not one per change. Each write saved is about 1 ms in each hidden page plus about 9 ms in the page in front and in Safari, and less for a suspended page to catch up on when it's shown.
- **Risks:**
  - A hidden tab's row shows a new title up to about 2.3 s late, and a new icon or fragment up to 300 ms late.
  - A background that stops with a write waiting loses it: a title, an icon, a fragment or a load, never an address. At its next start it syncs with the open tabs (`resync`), which writes them again, and Safari sends titles and icons again as tabs load after a restart. Safari stops a background after much longer than 2 s without events.
  - Timers in the background can fire late. In the WebKit host the 2 s write came about 0.7 s late once. The wait is then longer, never shorter, and any other write still takes the changes along.
- **Verified:** unit tests 434 (7 new, in `tests/tab-updates.test.js`): changes of a hidden tab gathered into one write, the title alone written at most every 2 s, the tab in front and an unknown tab written at once, a switch carrying the waiting change in its own write with nothing written after, a tab closed with a change waiting staying closed, a change with nothing new not writing the waiting ones, and `stateRev` while changes wait, while a write is on its way and after a failed one. With HEAD's `store.js` (it ignores `within`), 6 of the 7 fail. These mutations each fail a test: `within` ignored, the old `stateRev`, an empty change writing the waiting ones, a waiting key not marked on its way by a write made at once, no title limit, the tab in front waiting. Two guards don't fail a test: a waiting change behind a failed write is already covered by the failed write's own unknown state, and clearing the timer in `flush` only saves an empty wake. New WebKit host scenario `titles`: a hidden tab's title set 8 times in half a second. This build: 2 writes, and the row ends on the last title. HEAD's build: 8 writes. The tab in front's title shows in about 110 ms in both. `run.sh all`: 66 of 68 passed (load average 3–8). The host crashed in `introduce` (Trace/BPT trap), which passed alone. `press` failed its late checks. Run alone at load average 5–10 it failed 5 of 5 times on this build and 3 of 4 on HEAD's, with the switch landing 0.4–3 s after the press on both (near the host's background stopping). A switch asked from the page at 1.5 s, 3 runs of each build alternated, took 13–17 ms on this build and 13–22 ms on HEAD's, each preparation `known: 1`. So the press failures follow the load and the host's timing, not this change.
- **Review:**
  - **Clock set back (fixed).** The title limit counted with `Date.now()`. With the clock set back 60 s, a hidden tab's title waited about 60 s. It now uses `performance.now()`, and so does `store.js` for the delayed write's own time. With `Date.now()` there, a clock set forward kept a 300 ms change waiting behind a 2 s one.
  - **Address lost on a quit or update (fixed).** A scratch check of `syncTabs` with a renumbered tab: a stored address that's current keeps the tab in its space, and a stale one puts it in the window's active space. Before this slice the address was written within milliseconds. A hidden tab's new address is now written at once (see Changed). A page loading behind costs two writes instead of one.
  - **Tests that time real timers (fixed).** With a shim that fires `store.js`'s timers 200 ms late, 4 of the 7 tests failed on a correct build. They now wait for an expected write for up to 1.5 s. Checks that nothing is written yet stay timed, because a late timer can only make them pass. The tests pass with timers 200 ms and 500 ms late.
- **Verified after review:** unit tests 436 (9 in `tests/tab-updates.test.js`, 2 new: the clock set back and forward, and a hidden tab's new address). Both new tests fail on the code before these fixes, and 7 of 9 fail with HEAD's `store.js`. These mutations each fail a test: `Date.now()` for the title limit, `Date.now()` for the delayed write's time, no address rule, comparing raw addresses (a fragment written at once), the address taken from the event only, no title limit, the tab in front waiting. New WebKit host scenario `addresses`: a hidden tab's `pushState` is stored at once and a new fragment about 300 ms later. It passed 3 of 3 on this build and failed 2 of 2 on a build without the address rule. That build stored the address at about 330 ms, which a first, looser version of the check let through. The host looks every 100 ms, so the check now allows about 200 ms. `run.sh all`: 67 of 69 passed. `unloadedlate` failed once and passed alone 2 of 2 on this build and on HEAD's. `press` failed the same checks as before ("in front with the button still down", "the release asked for no second switch") 2 of 2 alone on both builds.

## Cheap page fixes: style reads not repeated, lists that don't grow, the zoom kept (2026-11-02)

- **Asked (Tirth):** lower CPU and memory use in the tab in front, in Safari and Chrome.
- **Why:** the round-3 code audit found four small costs that repeat.
  - `layout.js syncPlaceholder` read the style of `<html>::after` on every render, to see if the first-paint placeholder was there. A style read makes the page work out its styles first if anything changed. With the placeholder there, the first read switches it off and the reads stop. With none, they never stop: in Chrome's full-screen-only mode (Chrome's default, where nothing is registered), and in Safari with no frame and no Keep open.
  - `in-view.js outsideBody` walks from an observed element up to `<body>`, reading each one's style. It runs every time a page's IntersectionObserver hears different answers from the window and from `<body>`, which is every threshold crossing of anything fixed. The audit counted 5,760 style reads in 240 frames on an app layout.
  - `handlers.js getZoom` read `pageZoom` from storage and asked for the tab's zoom settings on every ask. Pages ask as they start, on every resize and every time they're shown.
  - `media.js` lists, `in-view.js` stand-ins and `sheets.js` moved `@media` rules were only cleared of what the page dropped when the box rested or `<body>` changed. On a page that makes many and keeps the box still, they grew for as long as the tab lived. `sheets.js` holds its rules strongly, so the rules of replaced sheets stayed in memory too.
- **Changed:**
  - `syncPlaceholder` asks once per `<html>`, then at most every 5 s (`PLACEHOLDER_RECHECK_MS`). It also asks at the first render after the page is hidden and when it's shown. A render in sight within the 5 s asks once they're over. It still asks again because WebKit applies newly registered CSS to pages already open: in the host, a page opened before Margin was installed had the placeholder after the install. A Margin update can register it after its pages have started. When the settings the placeholder is registered from change (Keep open, the frame, the width, the theme, full screen only), the page sets `data-margin-docked="off"` without asking. The new placeholder would arrive after the change.
  - `outsideBody` keeps each element's answer for 1 s (`OUTSIDE_FOR_MS`), in a WeakMap. It forgets all of them when the box moves, comes on or goes off, or `<body>` changes. A target whose answer came from a kept walk is chosen again once that walk runs out, and the page hears it if that changed what it was told. So something that turns fixed, a sticky header say, gets the right answer within a second, even if nothing crosses a threshold again.
  - `getZoom` keeps the stored `pageZoom` and each tab's default zoom in the background for up to 60 s (`ZOOM_KEEP_MS`). An unchanged zoom costs only the browser's answer. It forgets the stored zoom when `pageZoom` changes (`storage.onChanged`, or its own write), and the default zooms when the browser reports a zoom change (`tabs.onZoomChange`, where the browser has it). A read that started before a change isn't kept. What's kept only tells that nothing changed. A write reads storage first and builds on that. The 60 s limit is for copies of Margin, which don't hear each other's writes. Both listeners run in every copy, not only the one in charge, so a copy that takes charge again starts from what's stored. The times use `performance.now()`, a clock that only runs forward. The zoom itself is still asked of the browser every time.
  - The three lists are also swept whenever they hold twice as many as after their last sweep, starting at 64. The sweep drops what the page let go (`WeakRef`s), and rules no longer in a sheet on the page. It's the same test as when the box rests.
  - Chrome: the same code. Its full-screen-only mode gains most from the placeholder change.
- **Expected:** fewer forced style updates in the tab in front: one placeholder read per 5 s at most instead of one per render where there's no placeholder, and one walk per fixed element per second instead of one per threshold crossing. No storage read for a zoom ask that changed nothing. Lists that stay small on long-lived app tabs.
- **Risks:**
  - A site that strips `data-margin-docked` from `<html>` shows the placeholder's frame until a render, and then for up to 5 s more if the page is in sight (before: until the next render).
  - A placeholder registered late reaches a page in sight up to 5 s after the render that would have caught it. A hidden page catches it when it's shown.
  - An element that turns fixed, or stops being fixed, may get the wrong root for up to about 1 s: it can be reported in view or out of view for that long.
  - A copy of Margin that writes `pageZoom` while another copy keeps it can skip a write for up to 60 s: the other copy's kept map says nothing changed. A later ask after that writes it. Pages then draw Margin at an old zoom until the browser's answer, a moment later, as they do on a first visit. No write loses another copy's sites.
- **Verified:** unit tests 442 (6 new: 5 in `tests/page-upkeep.test.js`, against a small fake of the DOM, and 1 in `tests/spaces-switch.test.js`). All 6 fail on HEAD's code, and the existing zoom test now names the zoom change it simulates. These mutations each fail a test: no forgetting on a box move, no age limit on the walk, no `off` on a settings change, no recheck after 5 s, the placeholder answer not tied to its `<html>`, no sweep in `sheets.js`, `zoomChanged` or `zoomStored` doing nothing. WebKit host, scratch scenarios, 2 runs of each build, alternated with HEAD's build:
  - A fixed bar of 40 observed items crossing the window's edge every frame for 3 s: 8,648 style reads on HEAD's build, 264 on this one, with the same 181 callbacks and 3,982 entries for the page. The page's styles were clean when the observer ran, so the reads themselves took 2–4 ms in all on HEAD's build (at the page clock's 1 ms resolution). The saving is larger on a page whose styles change between reads.
  - 200 sidebar opens with no placeholder registered (copies of both builds with the placeholder files emptied): 200 placeholder reads on HEAD's build, 0 on this one. Opening took about 20 ms for the 200 on both, on a small test page.
  - 40 `getZoom` asks: under 1 ms each on both builds. The host's storage is fast, so the saving doesn't show there.
  - `run.sh all` (load average 4–5): 66 of 69 passed. `unseennav` and `slowunloaded` failed once and passed alone on this build and on HEAD's. `press` failed "the release asked for no second switch" alone on both builds, as in the slices before.
- **Review:**
  - **A kept walk could leave a page's answer wrong for good (fixed).** The kept answer is only looked at when a new entry comes. Something walked while in the flow, then floated with `position: fixed` within the second, would be told it's out of view, and nothing new would come while it stays in view. The reverse could also happen. A target whose answer came from a kept walk is now chosen again as that walk runs out (one timer per observer), and the page hears it if the answer changed. The review's unit scenario failed before the fix and passes now. In the host the scenario's first step didn't happen: `<body>` scrolls, so it clips an element in the flow for the window's observer too, and the two observers agreed there. The fix stays because it costs at most the walk the second saved. The host's fixed-bar measure still makes 264 style reads, the same as before the fix.
  - **A zoom write could drop another copy's sites (fixed).** The write was built from the kept `pageZoom`. Another copy's write within the 60 s, which this copy never hears of, was lost for good. A write now reads storage first and builds on that. The kept map only answers "nothing changed". It can still skip a write for up to 60 s (see Risks). A write costs one more storage read.
  - **A late placeholder was missed when the page was shown (fixed).** A page restored behind after a relaunch or an update can render before Safari brings its registrations back. Shown within 5 s, its show render skipped the check, so the placeholder's frame stayed on the page. It's now asked again at the first render after the page is hidden, when it's shown, and, for a page in sight, once the 5 s are over. In the host the registrations were there with the first render, after an install and after a relaunch, so this is covered by unit tests only.
  - **Verified after review:** unit tests 444 (2 new: the floated player in `tests/page-upkeep.test.js`, a placeholder registered late, shown, prepared hidden and in sight; the zoom test gains another copy's write). Each new check fails on the code before these fixes. These mutations each fail a test: no recheck of a kept walk, no fresh read before a zoom write, no reset when hidden or shown, no ask when shown, no ask once the 5 s are over, the late ask scheduled while hidden. `run.sh all` (load average 3–4): 66 of 69 passed. `behindlate` stopped with the host's Trace/BPT crash and passed alone 2 of 2. `slowunloaded` ("shown once it had painted") failed alone 4 of 4 on this build and 3 of 4 on HEAD's. `press` failed "the release asked for no second switch" alone on both builds.

## Round 3's last fixes: a restarted background knows its tabs, a tab resumed behind is kicked, holds of the tab in front aren't lost (2026-11-02)

- **Asked (Tirth):** switching as fast as plain Safari, no dark frames, less CPU and memory, with no new problems.
- **Why:** the reviews of round 3 found four real gaps, plus three small ones.
  - **A restarted background didn't kick.** Safari stops Margin's idle background within a minute. When a tab was left (`keepReadyLeftAt`) and which holds run (`keepReadyHolds`) are kept in storage.session for that reason. But only keep-ready's tick read them, 1.5 s after the first sync. The click that starts a stopped background is often to a tab left minutes ago, which Safari has likely suspended. That switch found nothing known about the tab, so it wasn't kicked and didn't use the stuck fallback. It waited 400 ms for the wake and up to 900 ms for the preparation, about 0.9 s, and then showed dark if the resume was slower. Two reviewers found this separately.
  - **A tab resumed behind was shown dark.** A hover wake, a press, the palette, a key run's tab woken ahead or keep-ready's first hold can resume a suspended tab at background priority before the switch. Its wake then answers, so the switch took the tab as awake: no kick, no paint barrier, and the preparation cut at 250 ms. In Tirth's Safari log such tabs still had their content hidden at the selection though they had resumed 0.9–2.6 s before. They showed Safari's empty view for 141–360 ms. Only a visible state clears that hide, which is what the kick gives.
  - **A hold that reached its page once the tab was in front was lost.** A first hold sent to a suspended tab waits behind its resume. A click shows the tab first, so the hold starts on a visible page and doesn't end (keepAwake only ends on the page coming into view, not on finding it there). The next tick, with the tab still in front, threw its key away. Once the tab was left, nothing could name that hold, and it ran its 75 s at foreground priority outside the six.
  - Small ones. A wake whose permission check took longer than 400 ms (Safari starting) turned the kick and the stuck fallback off for that switch. When tabs were left was kept for closed tabs too while keep-ready was off, and written whole at each switch. And the tab left is taken from the state, which can lag behind an activation queued behind a write: then the tab really left kept an older leave time, and looked suspended when it had been in front a second before.
- **Changed:**
  - keep-ready reads storage.session as the background starts (`sessionKnown`), and a switch waits for that read first, 200 ms at most. It's one read per background run, and nothing once it has answered. Chrome reads nothing (it keeps no tabs ready).
  - A wake answered more than 60 ms after its scripts went out marks its tab (`resumedBehind`): its process had been suspended. A switch to a marked tab kicks it even though its wake says it's awake, then gives it the paint barrier as any kicked tab. The mark goes when the tab is shown, kicked or introduced. A switch's own late wake was already kicked. The switch log's `kicked` has `behind: true` for these.
  - keep-ready keeps the sent, unexpired hold keys of the tab in front, in place of forgetting them. They're named once the tab is behind and not kept: at the next tick, or when keep-ready stops (turned off, or Safari unused for the window), as the next switch's tick stops again.
  - A wake still in its permission check at 400 ms counts as unanswered: the switch kicks the tab, or shows it 150 ms later.
  - Closed tabs' leave times are dropped at every tick, also with keep-ready off.
  - A tab shown counts as left no earlier than its show. A missed leave can only make a tab look hidden for less time than it was, so it waits the 60 ms before a kick instead of being kicked at once.
- **Not changed:** keepAwake still doesn't end when it starts on a visible page. The hold Margin places on the tab just left can reach the page before its visibility change does, and would then end at once, losing the most useful hold. The tab left is still taken from the state, so `holdNow` can hold the wrong tab in that race (as before round 3). A keep-ready first hold that resumed a tab behind, followed by a quick wake there, isn't marked: no answer comes back from a hold until it ends.
- **Risks:**
  - A busy page that answers a hover wake late, though its process was running, is kicked on the next switch: a flip, a second wake and a barrier, about 100–300 ms slower, the same as a switch whose own wake is late.
  - The first switch after a background start waits for the session read, a few ms normally and 200 ms at most.
  - A key kept for the tab in front stays until the tab is left and a tick runs. It isn't sent anywhere while the tab is in front.
- **Verified:** unit tests 451 (7 new, in `tests/wake.test.js` and `tests/keep-ready.test.js`). Each fails when its fix is undone: no wait for the session read, no kick for a tab resumed behind, the slow permission check taken as an answer, the active tab's keys dropped (2 tests), no prune with keep-ready off, and no lower bound at a show. The wake tests' fake storage.session now reads several keys at once, as Safari's does. `run.sh all` (load average about 4): 67 of 69 passed. `unseen` failed once and passed alone 2 of 2 on this build and on bed58e8's. `press` failed "the release asked for no second switch" alone on both builds, as in the slices before. Round 3 against bed58e8's build in the host, interleaved, medians of 4–5 runs: 10 hidden pages still held 2 s after a burst of 10 switches before, 6 now. Restores came up at 243 ms against 308. Loaded and never-shown switches were at the host's floor on both (11 and 82 ms against 12 and 85). Scripts per switch: 4 for a single switch on both, 4.4 against 4.0 in a burst (the releases). A hidden tab ticking its title every 250 ms: 2 state writes in 3.3 s against 7. The host can't suspend processes, so the kick paths are covered by unit tests only.

## No more asking pages for their animations (2026-11-03)

- **Why:** Tirth's Safari crashed the tab in front three times on 2026-11-02 (10:39, 11:37, 22:18), each time with the same signature: EXC_BAD_ACCESS in WebKit's `compareStyleOriginatedAnimationOwningElementPositionsInDocumentTreeOrder`, under `compareAnimationsByCompositeOrder`, `Document::matchingAnimations` and `document.getAnimations()`. The calls came from a requestAnimationFrame callback twice and from a dispatched event once, the two ways Margin reads `<body>` (`content/page/box.js`). The screen went dark for about 1.8 s while Safari reloaded the page. The signature first shows after 0973822, which added `document.getAnimations()` to every read. `Element.getAnimations()`, used to see whether a length was on its way, goes through the same sort. The bug is WebKit's, but Margin's calls reached it.
- **Changed:**
  - Nothing in `extension/` calls `getAnimations` any more. A unit test fails if anything does.
  - **What's on its way is known from events** (`content/page/moves.js`). `transitionrun` and `animationstart` on `<html>`, `<body>` and the first children add to a list. What's on it counts if it moves something a read looks at. Their `transitionend`, `transitioncancel`, `animationend` and `animationcancel` take it off. A read with something on the list waits, and runs again once the list is empty, as before. Listeners are on the window, in the capture phase, and only trusted events count.
  - An event comes a frame after its transition started, so a read can miss one that has just started. Whatever starts is read again as it ends.
  - An animation counts only if it has an end, runs, and its `@keyframes` set something a read looks at. The keyframes are looked up in the sheets the page can read. If none has them (another site's sheet), the animation counts.
  - What left the page drops off the list. So does anything older than 10 s, in case its end never comes.
  - **A first child that has just come** is counted from its first start. Starts are kept for anything up to 8 levels under `<body>`, as deep as the first children go, and counted once a read looks at the element. A banner put first in `<body>` in a frame's callbacks starts moving in the next frame's events, before Margin's next look at the first children. Before this, its start was thrown away, the read caught its margin part way, and the page's top margin stayed lost (review, both engines).
  - What ends is read again if a read looks at it by then, so one found by a read itself on its way is read again too. An animation no longer named in `animation-name` doesn't count.
  - **A read's own transitions** in what inherits from `<body>` are now seen as events in the next frame. Each comes as a `transitionrun` and, at 0 s, a `transitioncancel` (Chromium) or a `transitionend` (WebKit), for the same element and property, in the frame after a read. A page's own transition never does that. Such a pair isn't the page moving, so nothing is read again for it. It sets `held` in both engines, so later reads start none. Before, `held` was set in the read itself. Without this, WebKit read again at each of those ends, each read started the same transitions, and a page with `margin: inherit` and a transition on a first child was read 5 times a second for as long as it was open (review).
  - **What no event tells of**: an animation a script starts with `element.animate()` sends none. A read the page's own changes asked for, which found something new the page says, is made once more 1 s later. If that one finds something new again, another follows, 3 at most in a row. A read for a move of the box or a resize starts none: the box's width is left out of what counts as new. Before, a script's slide of `<body>`'s margin or of a new first child's was read at its start and kept (review).
  - **The page's style is brought up to date before a read**, with `checkVisibility()` on `<html>`. `document.getAnimations()` did that as a side effect. Without it, WebKit restyled what was waiting under `<body>` with the read's elements in, and not again once they were gone. The host's flex column (`body-size` F and O) kept its children shrunk. `getComputedStyle` isn't enough: it only brings the element asked for and its ancestors up to date. On 20,000 rows `checkVisibility()` cost the same as `getAnimations()` (25 ms in WebKit and 11 in Chromium with style to redo, 0 without) and no layout (59 and 27 ms).
- **Checked:**
  - Headless WebKit and Chromium, the page bundle at HEAD and now, side by side: `<body>`'s margin in a transition lands at 300, a margin animation at 120, both builds and both engines. A child with `padding: inherit` and a transition: HEAD's Chromium build sends it 8 transition events on the first late read and none after. This build sends none in either engine.
  - Host, before the review's fixes: `late-body`, `read-limits`, `late-reads`, `read-shape` and `body-size` all pass. `body-size` failed F and O before the style update was added, and passed on HEAD's build.
  - The review's pages, headless, before and after its fixes, in both engines. A first child put in by a frame's callback, or in a task with its style forced, sliding its margin in by an animation or a transition: the gap above the page was lost (0 px) and is now kept (40 px, as at HEAD). `margin: inherit` and a transition on a first child, in WebKit: 25 reads in 5 idle seconds before, 1 now (the settling read), none after. A script's `animate()` of `<body>`'s margin, and of a new first child's: read part way before, read at its end now.
- **Risks:**
  - A transition started in the same frame as a read is read once at its start and again at its end. That's one more read than before for such a transition.
  - A transition of a length on `<html>`, `<body>` or a first child is followed by a read as it ends, even if no read ran during it. Rare on those elements.
  - On a page that inherits from `<body>` in Chromium, if a child's transition from an earlier change is still running when the first late read comes, that transition plays out instead of jumping to its end. Before, `held` was set within the read.
  - An animation whose play state is changed from paused to running later isn't seen, since no new `animationstart` comes.
  - A read the page asked for that found something new costs one more read a second later, sometimes up to 3. A script's animation of these lengths that runs longer than about 3 s is still read part way.
  - Starts on anything up to 8 levels under `<body>` are kept until they end. Endless animations among them are swept once more than 64 elements are on the list, or as a read looks.
  - A page that stops transition events at the window before Margin's listener (only possible when Margin is injected late) holds reads back up to 10 s.
- **Verified:** unit tests 461 (10 in `tests/page-moves.test.js`). The check that nothing calls `getAnimations` fails on HEAD's `box.js`, and the rest fail on HEAD, where `moves.js` doesn't exist. The review's three (a first child that has just come, a read's own transitions, the settling read) fail on the version reviewed. Host, alone: `read-limits`, `late-reads`, `read-shape` and `body-size` pass. `read-limits` P failed while a move of the box could start a settling read, so the box's width is left out of what counts as new. `late-body` fails M, O and T "in its box at 360" alone on this build, on the version reviewed and on HEAD's (the sidebar width change never comes), and passes within `run.sh all`. `run.sh all` (load average about 3): 67 of 69 passed. `update` A and `catchup-loaded` B failed. Run alone, both passed on this build and on the version reviewed, `catchup-loaded` 5 of 5 on this build.

## Keep-ready made light: the tab just left, for a minute (2026-11-03)

- **Asked (Tirth):** with keep-ready at 0 he sees big black flashes. He wants them gone without giving up battery and memory.
- **Why:** his Safari on 2026-11-02 (round 4's diagnosis). Keep-ready held about six heavy hidden tabs at foreground priority all the time: up to six tabs of the space in front, renewed every minute until 30 minutes after Safari was last used. Any click, key or scroll renewed the 30 minutes. Held hidden tabs used 60–70% of all page CPU, about 22% of a core each, and his battery went from 45% to 6% in an hour. With keep-ready off, every switch back lands on a tab Safari has suspended, and that shows dark. Earlier rounds showed tabs held awake were never dark (0 in about 60), and most returns are to a tab used a moment ago. So the hold is worth keeping, but only for the tab you're most likely to go back to, and only for a short time.
  - Two smaller problems: the keep-ready alarm started Margin's background once a minute in his other Safari profiles for nothing. And a restarted background didn't know the holds the one before had placed, so it held six more beside them.
- **Changed** (`background/keep-ready.js`, `background.js`, `background/handlers.js`, `shared/away.js`, the new-tab page's Settings):
  - Only the tab just left is held, once, for the setting's minutes (default 1, at most 10). Nothing is renewed. The space's other tabs aren't held, and a background start holds nothing.
  - At most 2 are held: the tab just left and the one left before it. A third lets go of the one left longest ago at once. Which one goes is decided by when each tab was left (the tab events), not by the state's last use. The state can lag behind a run of quick switches. A tab let go is released even if the state still shows it in front, as long as the tab events say it was left. In the host, a fast run of switches otherwise kept a tab two switches back running as if it were in front.
  - The tab left is taken from the activations as they come (`frontChanged`), not from the state. The state's `active` lags while an activation's write waits behind the one under way. Through A → B → C in quick succession it still had A in front at C's activation, so A was held a second time and B, the tab just left, never was. The state is used only for a window this background hasn't seen a switch in (it has just started). A tab closed or moved to another window is no longer in front of its own.
  - When Safari is left for another app, every hold ends 3 s later, and none is placed until Safari is used again. Coming back doesn't hold anything again. Two signals say Safari was left:
    - `windows.onFocusChanged` with `WINDOW_ID_NONE`. WebKit sends this when the browser tells it no window has focus (`WebExtensionContext::didFocusWindow(nullptr)`, checked in WebKit's source). Whether Safari does that when it's deactivated isn't known.
    - The page in front loses focus while it's still in sight (`shared/away.js`: `blur`, then `document.hasFocus()` false and the page visible). It sends `away` once, and `back` when it gets focus again. A page hidden as it loses focus doesn't count, since that's a switch to another tab.
    - With focus in a frame of the page (Google Docs, an embedded editor, a payment form), leaving Safari blurs only that frame's window, and Margin's scripts run in the top frame only. So while focus is in a frame, the page asks `document.hasFocus()` once a second, and says `away` when it turns false. After `away` it asks the same way for up to 5 s, so focus coming back inside a frame still sends `back`. Past that, the next switch says Safari is in use anyway.
    - A page's `away` comes through more hops than a window's focus or an activation, so it can arrive after them, and then nothing would cancel its 3 s wait. It carries when the page lost focus. It doesn't count if Safari was seen in use after that time (a tab switch), if it comes from a window other than the one focused last (a click into another Safari window), or from a tab no longer in front.
    - The 3 s wait covers a glance at another app and straight back, and a `WINDOW_ID_NONE` that may come between two Safari windows. A window focused, a tab switch or `back` cancels it.
  - The 'used' messages are gone (a page sent one at most once a minute on a click, key or scroll). Nothing needs to know when Safari was last used any more.
  - No keep-ready alarm. Nothing is renewed, so nothing needs to wake the background. If an alarm from the old build still fires, it's cleared, in any copy or profile.
  - A background that starts again reads the holds still running from `storage.session`. They count toward the 2 and are released with the rest.
  - A closed tab is forgotten at once (`tabs.onRemoved`): its holds, when it was left, and whether it had a page.
  - **The setting:** `keepTabsReadyMinutes` is now `keepLeftTabReadyMinutes`. It's labelled "Keep the tab you just left ready to show, with no flash, for this long (minutes, 0 = off; let go when you leave Safari)", from 0 to 10. A stored old value doesn't carry over, not even 0: the new one is 1 until it's saved. Tirth set the old one to 0 to stop what it cost, and asked for the flashes gone without that cost, which is what the new one is. Keeping his 0 would have kept every flash. Pages read the settings as stored, so they apply the same default (`shared/view.js` `keepReadyMinutes`), and Settings shows what the background does. A downgrade to an older build would see no old setting and turn keep-ready on at 30.
- **Expected:** at most 2 hidden tabs held, each for a minute after it was left, and none while Safari isn't in front or once switching stops. Before, about 6 were held all the time. Battery and CPU should be close to keep-ready off. Switching straight back to the tab just left, or to the one before it, within a minute should show no flash. A tab left longer ago than that is suspended, and switching to it is the kick and paint path, as with keep-ready off.
- **Not changed:** the kick, the stuck path and the space switcher (other slices). Hover wakes still hold the tab under the pointer for their own few seconds.
- **Risks:**
  - Clicking into Safari's own address bar or a Safari sheet also blurs the page. If focus doesn't come back to the page within 3 s, the holds end, and going back to the tab just left can flash.
  - A page's `away` is ignored when it comes from a window other than the one focused last. That relies on `windows.onFocusChanged` with window ids, which Safari may not send. Without it, a click into another Safari window can still end the holds of the first one 3 s later.
  - With focus in a frame, leaving Safari is noticed up to a second late, and a page that keeps focus in a frame runs a 1 s check while it's in front.
  - If Safari doesn't send `WINDOW_ID_NONE`, and the window is hidden (⌘H, minimized) without the page seeing a blur while it's visible, the holds run out their minute.
  - A second tab held costs one more hidden process at foreground priority for the minute after a switch. Set `MAX_HELD` to 1 if his log shows going back two tabs is rare.
  - A tab more than two switches back, or left more than a minute ago, flashes when switched to, as with keep-ready off.
- **Verified:** unit tests 452 (21 in `tests/keep-ready.test.js`, rewritten for the new behaviour; 1 new in `tests/model.test.js`, which also checks the pages' reading against the background's; 2 in the new `tests/away.test.js`). Against HEAD's code (with names added for the new calls), 13 of the keep-ready tests fail: only the tab left held, its length, never renewed, at most two, no alarm, when Safari is left (3), a restarted background, a closed tab, the switch log facts and the two stale-state cases. The other 8 are behaviour kept from before and pass on both. The model test and `away.test.js` fail on HEAD. Mutations each fail a test: no `heldUntil` from storage.session, no 3 s wait, holding while away, three held, a closed tab keeping its leave time, away releasing nothing, the state's `active` trusted, and the state's last use for the order. WebKit host: `keep-ready-cap` changed to two held of nine switches, and a new `keep-ready-away`. Both pass, but under a load average of 13–24 they sometimes failed: a release landed 1.5–3 s late, near the end of the host's ~5 s of background life. HEAD's `keep-ready-cap` never released anything in that run (the switches before the first sync aren't held, so it held 4 of 6), so it can't be compared. The release checks now wait 3 s. `run.sh all` (load average 4–8): 68 of 70 passed. `unseennav` failed once and passed alone 2 of 2 on this build and on HEAD's. `press` failed "the release asked for no second switch" alone on both builds, as in the slices before. After the last changes, `keep-ready-cap` and `keep-ready-away` passed alone. The host can't suspend processes or take focus away, so CPU, battery and the real focus signals are for Tirth's Safari (TESTING.md).
- **Review fixes, verified:** unit tests 456. New: the tab left from the activations with the state behind, a page's `away` that doesn't count (three cases), the frame watch (2 in `tests/away.test.js`), and the old setting's 0 not carried over. Each fails on the version reviewed. With the state's `active`, the quick-switch test held tab 1 twice and tab 2 never. Each of the three `away` checks is the only one that rejects its case in the test. `keep-ready-away` now also sends `away` from a tab behind, 1.2 s before the real one: it passes on this build and fails "still held 2 s after Safari was left" on the reviewed version's (MARGIN_HOST_DIST). `run.sh all` (load average 3–9): 69 of 70 passed, `keep-ready-cap` and `keep-ready-away` among them. `press` failed "in front with the button still down" and "the release asked for no second switch" alone on this build and on HEAD's (git archive), as before.
